Comparison with the pioneer data-driven approach

Let us consider the very particular situation where strain and stress are uniform for the family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{N}_{test}$$\end{document}Ntest of tested structures. It follows that one can easily define the Experimental Constitutive Manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\Gamma }_{exp}$$\end{document}Γexp:55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} { \varvec{\Gamma }_{exp} = \left\{ \left( \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }^K(i),\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S(i)\right) \in \textbf{R}^6 \times \textbf{R}^6, i \in \textbf{N}_{test}\right\} } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γexp=ϵϵK(i),σσS(i)∈R6×R6,i∈NtestThe pioneer work [41] starts with the Experimental Constitutive Manifold which is in general not explicitly defined in terms of experimental data. To get the data-driven material model, an interpolation operator is added, for instance using a nearest neighbors (k-NN) algorithm.The present work is different in the sense that from materials science, the available mathematical shapes of the Experimental Constitutive Manifold are given.

For linear elastic materials, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\Gamma }_{exp}$$\end{document}Γexp should be close to a linear manifold that is computed thanks to the CRE. One gets for tests covering the strain and stress spaces:56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} { \mathbb {K}= [\mathbb {Q}^K]^{-1/2} \left( [\mathbb {Q}^K]^{1/2} \mathbb {Q}^S [\mathbb {Q}^K]^{1/2}\right) ^{1/2}[\mathbb {Q}^K]^{-1/2} } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K=[QK]-1/2[QK]1/2QS[QK]1/21/2[QK]-1/2with57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} { \mathbb {Q}^K = \frac{1}{N_{test}}\sum _{i \in \textbf{N}_{test}} \underline{\epsilon }^K(i)\underline{\epsilon }^K(i)^T ; \quad \mathbb {Q}^S = \frac{1}{N_{test}}\sum _{i \in \textbf{N}_{test}} \underline{\sigma }^S(i) \underline{\sigma }^S(i)^T } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}QK=1Ntest∑i∈Ntestϵ_K(i)ϵ_K(i)T;QS=1Ntest∑i∈Ntestσ_S(i)σ_S(i)TIn the case of nonlinear but stable elastic materials, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\Gamma }_{exp}$$\end{document}Γexp is nonlinear and should be close to a manifold defined by the convex potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi \in \uppsi $$\end{document}ψ∈ψ. Data-driven material modeling—stable elasto-(visco-)plastic materials

The CRE-method is now set up for the data-driven modeling of stable elasto-(visco-)plastic materials. There are two key questions for which a solution is given:the mathematical shape of the most general data-driven model compatible with knowledge from physics and materials science;the computation of standard ill-posed problems for elasto-(visco-)plastic materials.

Experimental data

We use similar notations as in the previous sections, but introducing now the time domain. We have a series of tested structures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \textbf{N}_{test}$$\end{document}i∈Ntest over the time interval [0, T]. Data obtained from measurements is:59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{U}_d(i) \; \text {over}\, \partial _1 \Omega \times [0,T] ; \quad \underline{F}_d(i) \; \text {over}\, \partial _2 \Omega \times [0,T] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U_d(i)over∂1Ω×[0,T];F_d(i)over∂2Ω×[0,T]The i-problems (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \textbf{N}_{test}$$\end{document}i∈Ntest) are standard ill-posed problems for which the admissible spaces for the displacement-stress pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\underline{U}^K,\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S)$$\end{document}(U_K,σσS) are denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\mathcal {U}}_{ad}^{K,[0,T]}$$\end{document}UadK,[0,T] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\mathcal {S}}_{ad}^{S,[0,T]}$$\end{document}SadS,[0,T].

Mathematical shape of data-driven models

The goal in this section is to derive the mathematical shape of the most general available model, the elastic stiffness being known. We use here the internal variables framework which is very general and common in materials science; this is the basis of the material modeling developed here for stable elasto-(visco-)plastic materials.

The different internal variables

The state of the material at time t is defined by the value of the following set:60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_p,\textbf{X},\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\textbf{Y}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(ϵϵp,X,σσ,Y)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_p$$\end{document}ϵϵp (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }-\textbf{K}^{-1}\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }$$\end{document}=ϵϵ-K-1σσ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }$$\end{document}σσ are observable variables, while the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})$$\end{document}(X,Y) gathers hidden internal variables. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{Y}$$\end{document}Y is the force associated to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}$$\end{document}X and both belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}^q$$\end{document}Rq. In a data-driven mathematical model, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}$$\end{document}X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{Y}$$\end{document}Y are a priori unknown as well as their dimension q.

The classical formulation

For the sake of simplicity, one assumes isothermal conditions. Following the first principle of thermodynamics of irreversible processes, we consider a Helmholtz free energy e which depends on the state of the material defined by the elastic strain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e$$\end{document}ϵϵe, the hidden variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}\in \textbf{R}^q$$\end{document}X∈Rq and the cumulated inelastic strain p:61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e(\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e,\textbf{X},p) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e(ϵϵe,X,p)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}$$\end{document}X has often a precise interpretation; for example, in the case of kinematic hardening, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}$$\end{document}X is associated in the classical models to the center of the elasticity domain.

As classically done in materials science, one introduces the following decoupling hypothesis (H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}1) used in this section:62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e = \frac{1}{2}\text {Tr}[\textbf{K}\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e] + \psi (\textbf{X},p) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e=12Tr[Kϵϵeϵϵe]+ψ(X,p)It follows the state equations defining the forces associated to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e,\textbf{X},p)$$\end{document}(ϵϵe,X,p):63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }= \textbf{K}\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e ; \quad \textbf{Y}= \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial \textbf{X}} ; \quad R = \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial p} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σσ=Kϵϵe;Y=∂ψ∂X;R=∂ψ∂pThe dissipation is equal to:64\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \omega&= \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}] - \dot{e} \\&= \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p] - \dot{\psi } \\&= \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p] - \textbf{Y}\cdot \dot{\textbf{X}} - R \dot{p} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ω=Tr[σσϵϵ˙]-e˙=Tr[σσϵϵ˙p]-ψ˙=Tr[σσϵϵ˙p]-Y·X˙-Rp˙The second principle of thermodynamics of irreversible processes indicates that the dissipation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω is positive or null for all admissible histories of the material. To satisfy this constraint, one adds the state evolution laws:65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left[ \begin{array}{c}\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p \\ -\dot{\textbf{X}} \\ -\dot{p} \end{array} \right] = \textbf{B}\left( \left[ \begin{array}{c}\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\\ \textbf{Y}\\ R \end{array} \right] \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵϵ˙p-X˙-p˙=BσσYRwith \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_p=0$$\end{document}ϵϵp=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}=0$$\end{document}X=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=0$$\end{document}p=0 at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=0$$\end{document}t=0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{B}$$\end{document}B is positive.

The normal formulation

The hidden internal variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y}) \in \textbf{R}^q \times \textbf{R}^q$$\end{document}(X,Y)∈Rq×Rq are not intrinsic in the sense that another pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{X}',\textbf{Y}') \in \textbf{R}^q \times \textbf{R}^q$$\end{document}(X′,Y′)∈Rq×Rq can lead to the same observable quantities i.e. the same material model. This legitimates (as done in [47]) a choice more remarkable than the others, the so-called normal formulation. It results from a transformation of hidden variables, from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})$$\end{document}(X,Y) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{\textbf{Y}})$$\end{document}(X~,Y~), which leads to an equivalence between the kinematic and static variables, of the form:66\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\textbf{X}} = \varvec{\Lambda }\tilde{\textbf{Y}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X~=ΛY~where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\Lambda }$$\end{document}Λ is a positive symmetric constant linear operator. Here, we take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\Lambda }= \textbf{I}$$\end{document}Λ=I. This formulation is not unique, and the following property introduces a particular one where the only assumption is the decoupling hypothesis (H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}1). Moreover, this normal formulation preserves the energy and the dissipation.

Property 4

Let the Helmholtz free energy be:67\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e(\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e,\textbf{X},p) = \frac{1}{2}\text {Tr}[\textbf{K}\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e] + \psi (\textbf{X},p) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e(ϵϵe,X,p)=12Tr[Kϵϵeϵϵe]+ψ(X,p)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi $$\end{document}ψ is a convex positive function, twice differentiable such that:68\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi (\textbf{0},0)=0 ; \quad \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial \textbf{X}}(\textbf{0},0)=\textbf{0} ; \quad \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial p}(\textbf{0},0)=0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ(0,0)=0;∂ψ∂X(0,0)=0;∂ψ∂p(0,0)=0There is an internal variables transform:69\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\textbf{X}}=\textbf{Q}_v(\textbf{X},p) ; \quad \tilde{p} = q_v(\textbf{X},p) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X~=Qv(X,p);p~=qv(X,p)such that the energy can be written with the new variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})$$\end{document}(X~,p~):70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e = \frac{1}{2}\text {Tr}[\textbf{K}\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e] + \frac{1}{2}\tilde{\textbf{X}} \cdot \tilde{\textbf{X}} + \frac{1}{2}\tilde{p}^2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e=12Tr[Kϵϵeϵϵe]+12X~·X~+12p~2The dissipation is also preserved. The force \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{Y}}$$\end{document}Y~ associated to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{X}}$$\end{document}X~ is such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{Y}}=\tilde{\textbf{X}}$$\end{document}Y~=X~ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}$$\end{document}p~ can be taken in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}^+$$\end{document}R+.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{Z}=\left[ \begin{array}{c}\textbf{X}\\ p\end{array}\right] \in \textbf{R}^{q+1}$$\end{document}Z=Xp∈Rq+1 and71\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\psi }(\textbf{Z}) = \textbf{Z}^T\left[ \int _0^1 \text {d}\mu \int _0^1 \lambda \text {d}\lambda \frac{\partial ^2 \psi }{\partial \textbf{Z}^2}(\lambda \mu \textbf{Z})\right] \textbf{Z}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ¯(Z)=ZT∫01dμ∫01λdλ∂2ψ∂Z2(λμZ)ZFirst, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\psi }(\textbf{Z})=\psi (\textbf{Z})$$\end{document}ψ¯(Z)=ψ(Z). One has:72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\psi }(\textbf{Z}) = \int _0^1 \text {d}\mu \frac{\text {d}}{\text {d}\mu } \Gamma (\mu ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ¯(Z)=∫01dμddμΓ(μ)with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma (\mu ) = \int _0^1 \text {d}\lambda \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial \textbf{Z}}(\mu \lambda \textbf{Z}) \textbf{Z}$$\end{document}Γ(μ)=∫01dλ∂ψ∂Z(μλZ)Z. It follows:73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\psi }(\textbf{Z}) = \Gamma (1) - \Gamma (0) = \int _0^1 \text {d}\lambda \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial \textbf{Z}}(\lambda \textbf{Z}) \textbf{Z}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ¯(Z)=Γ(1)-Γ(0)=∫01dλ∂ψ∂Z(λZ)ZFinally, one has:74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\psi }(\textbf{Z}) = \psi (\textbf{Z}) - \psi (\textbf{0}) = \psi (\textbf{Z}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ¯(Z)=ψ(Z)-ψ(0)=ψ(Z)Let us introduce the operator75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{A}= 2 \int _0^1\int _0^1 \lambda \text {d}\lambda \text {d}\mu \frac{\partial ^2\psi }{\partial \textbf{Z}^2}(\lambda \mu \textbf{Z}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=2∫01∫01λdλdμ∂2ψ∂Z2(λμZ)It is symmetric and also positive, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi $$\end{document}ψ being a convex function. Consequently, one can define the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{A}^{1/2}$$\end{document}A1/2 and the new state variables are:76\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left[ \begin{array}{c}\tilde{\textbf{X}}\\ \tilde{p}\end{array}\right] = \textbf{A}^{1/2} \left[ \begin{array}{c}\textbf{X}\\ p\end{array}\right] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X~p~=A1/2Xpwith \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p}) = \frac{1}{2}\tilde{\textbf{X}}\cdot \tilde{\textbf{X}} + \frac{1}{2}\tilde{p}^2$$\end{document}ψ(X~,p~)=12X~·X~+12p~2.

The energy being preserved, it is also true for the dissipation. The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{B}$$\end{document}B which defines the state evolution laws is of course modified, the relation between the observable variables being preserved. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The normal formulation has serious advantages. For stable materials with a convex energy, it allows to divide by two the number of hidden internal variables and to get an energy parametrized by the Hooke tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{K}$$\end{document}K alone.

Elasto-viscoplasticity is characterized by the fact that the components of the generalized strain rate, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p$$\end{document}ϵϵ˙p, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}}$$\end{document}X~˙, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\tilde{p}}$$\end{document}p~˙, are null at the same time. Consequently, the most general mathematical model can be written:77\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p&= \textbf{g}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})\dot{\tilde{p}} \\ \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}}&= \textbf{h}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})\dot{\tilde{p}} \\ \dot{\tilde{p}}&= f(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p}) \ge 0 \\ \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_p&= 0, \tilde{\textbf{X}} = \textbf{0}, \tilde{p} = 0 \; \text {at} \, t=0 \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵϵ˙p=g(σσ,X~,p~)p~˙X~˙=h(σσ,X~,p~)p~˙p~˙=f(σσ,X~,p~)≥0ϵϵp=0,X~=0,p~=0att=0This model is defined by three functions and the number q of hidden variables. To fulfill the second principle of thermodynamics of irreversible processes, one should satisfy:78\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p] - \tilde{\textbf{X}} \cdot \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}} - \tilde{p}.\dot{\tilde{p}} \ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Tr[σσϵϵ˙p]-X~·X~˙-p~.p~˙≥0For elastoplastic materials for which the behavior is independent of the loading rate, the third equation of (77) is replaced by:79\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\tilde{p}} \overline{f}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}} \tilde{p}) = 0 ; \quad \dot{\tilde{p}}\ge 0 ; \quad \overline{f} \le 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p~˙f¯(σσ,X~p~)=0;p~˙≥0;f¯≤0

Remark 2

We have shown in [50] that the functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{g}$$\end{document}g, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{h}$$\end{document}h, f should be single-valued to get a consistent material model with (77) and (78). This univocity property is the basis of the construction method of the hidden internal state variables from data introduced in [24, 50]. Its interest is its generality. However, we will see that the CRE enables to solve the same problem with a more robust approach but less general in the sense that a CRE must be defined beforehand, constraint that we are considering today as relatively weak.

The subfamily of standard materials

The decoupling hypothesis (H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}1) and the convexity property of the energy e belong to commonly accepted assumptions in materials science as they have been validated by experiments for stable materials. Consequently, they will be systematically taken into account a priori in the following. Here, we go one step further considering stable materials which, for most of them, can be described by a standard material model. Such a family, very common in materials science, has been validated for numerous materials. It has been introduced by B. Halphen and Q.S. Nguyen [29].

Precisely, a standard material model is defined by two potentials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^*(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσ,X~,p~) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}})$$\end{document}ϕ(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙) which are two dual convex functions related to the dissipation bilinear form:80\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( (\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}}), (\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})\right) \in \textbf{R}^{6+q+1} \times \textbf{R}^{6+q+1} \longmapsto \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p]- \tilde{\textbf{X}}\cdot \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}} - \tilde{p}.\dot{\tilde{p}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙),(σσ,X~,p~)∈R6+q+1×R6+q+1⟼Tr[σσϵϵ˙p]-X~·X~˙-p~.p~˙One should also satisfy the second principle of thermodynamics of irreversible processes, that corresponds to:81\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\phi ^*}(0,\textbf{0},0) = 0 ; \quad \phi ^{*} \ge 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗(0,0,0)=0;ϕ∗≥0Such a material model depends only on one convex function and it is compatible with the two principles of thermodynamics. It also depends on the dimension q of the vector of hidden internal variables.

The local CRE then reads:82\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e^2_{CRE} = \phi (\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}}) + \phi ^*(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p}) - \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p]+ \tilde{\textbf{X}}\cdot \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}} + \tilde{p}.\dot{\tilde{p}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}eCRE2=ϕ(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙)+ϕ∗(σσ,X~,p~)-Tr[σσϵϵ˙p]+X~·X~˙+p~.p~˙and the global CRE over the time-space domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,T] \times \Omega $$\end{document}[0,T]×Ω is equal to:83\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E^2_{CRE} = \int _0^T \text {d}t\int _{\Omega }\text {d}\Omega \eta (t) e^2_{CRE} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ECRE2=∫0Tdt∫ΩdΩη(t)eCRE2where the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (t)$$\end{document}η(t) may be chosen as 1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-t/T)$$\end{document}(1-t/T).

Let us return to the normal formulation and the following question: is there a normal formulation for a standard initial model that retains the property of being standard? The answer has been given in [47]; one has:

Property 5

A standard material model defined by the convex potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\textbf{Z})$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσ,Z) and the inelastic part of the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (\textbf{Z})$$\end{document}ψ(Z) admits a normal standard formulation is there exists an operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}$$\end{document}R (defining a new set of hidden internal variables) such that:84\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\tilde{\textbf{Z}} \equiv \left[ \begin{array}{c}\tilde{\textbf{X}}\\ \tilde{p}\end{array}\right] = \textbf{R}(\textbf{Z}) \\&\left( \frac{\partial \tilde{\textbf{Z}}}{\partial \textbf{Z}}\right) ^T\left( \frac{\partial \tilde{\textbf{Z}}}{\partial \textbf{Z}}\right) = \frac{\partial }{\partial \textbf{Z}}\left( \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial \textbf{Z}}\right) ^T \\&{\phi ^*}\left( \sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\left( \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial \textbf{Z}}\right) ^T(\textbf{R}^{-1}(\tilde{\textbf{Z}}))\right) \; \text {is a convex function} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z~≡X~p~=R(Z)∂Z~∂ZT∂Z~∂Z=∂∂Z∂ψ∂ZTϕ∗σσ,∂ψ∂ZT(R-1(Z~))is a convex function

This normal formulation is different from the one introduced in “The normal formulation” section. The conditions of Property 5 are much more common than they appear at first. They are satisfied by classical parametrized models of materials science [47]; an illustration is given a little further on.

One can remark that the main condition:85\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \frac{\partial \tilde{\textbf{Z}}}{\partial \textbf{Z}}\right) ^T\left( \frac{\partial \tilde{\textbf{Z}}}{\partial \textbf{Z}}\right) = \frac{\partial }{\partial \textbf{Z}}\left( \frac{\partial \psi }{\partial \textbf{Z}}\right) ^T \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂Z~∂ZT∂Z~∂Z=∂∂Z∂ψ∂ZTinvolves a deformation. The right-hand side should be positive which implies the convexity of the energy. An approximation can be given if we neglect the square of the nonlinear part of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Finally, as a conclusion of the previous developments, we consider that the family of normal and standard models provides a reasonable framework for the data-driven modeling of stable elasto-(visco-)plastic materials.

A classical model in materials science

Let us consider first the standard version of the Chaboche-Marquis elasto-(visco-)plastic model (see [47, 52]). We will see that this model admits a version which is both standard and normal.

The material is supposed to be isotropic; the internal hidden variable is a second order tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {X}$$\end{document}X with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Tr}[\mathbb {X}]=0$$\end{document}Tr[X]=0, its associated force being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Y}$$\end{document}Y with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Tr}[\mathbb {Y}]=0$$\end{document}Tr[Y]=0. The dissipation potential in viscoplasticity is with classical notations:89\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D,\mathbb {Y},R) = \frac{k}{n+1}\langle z \rangle _+^{n+1} \quad n >0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσD,Y,R)=kn+1⟨z⟩+n+1n>0with90\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} z&= |\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D-\mathbb {Y}| + \frac{1}{2}a|\mathbb {Y}|^2 - R \\ \mathbb {Y}&= c \mathbb {X}\\ R&= \gamma (p) \\ p&= \int _0^T |\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p| \text {d}t\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}z=|σσD-Y|+12a|Y|2-RY=cXR=γ(p)p=∫0T|ϵϵ˙p|dtk, n, a, c are positive material parameters. The threshold function:91\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p \in \textbf{R}^+ \longmapsto \gamma (p) \in \textbf{R}^+ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p∈R+⟼γ(p)∈R+is also a material function which is supposed to be concave and increasing.

To get a normal standard formulation, let us introduce:92\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \tilde{\mathbb {X}}&= c^{1/2} \mathbb {X}\\ \tilde{p}&= \int _0^p \text {d}p [\frac{\partial \gamma }{\partial p}]^{1/2} \\ R&= \ell (\tilde{p}) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X~=c1/2Xp~=∫0pdp[∂γ∂p]1/2R=ℓ(p~)One has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\phi }^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D,\tilde{\mathbb {X}},\tilde{p})={\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D,c^{1/2} \mathbb {X},\ell (\tilde{p}))$$\end{document}ϕ~∗(σσD,X~,p~)=ϕ∗(σσD,c1/2X,ℓ(p~)) and one can check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\phi }^*}$$\end{document}ϕ~∗ is convex.

Standard material model for elastoplastic materials

Let us consider the case of elastoplastic materials. The potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^*$$\end{document}ϕ∗ is the indicator function of the elastic domain which is convex; one has:93\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi ^*(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})= {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0 \qquad \text {if}\; \overline{f}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p}) \le 0 \\ + \infty \quad \text {otherwise} \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσ,X~,p~)=0iff¯(σσ,X~,p~)≤0+∞otherwisewith \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}(0,0,0) \le 0$$\end{document}f¯(0,0,0)≤0.

This mathematical model depends also on one convex function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}$$\end{document}f¯. This function can be simplified because we know from materials science that the elasticity domain grows with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}$$\end{document}p~ which can be interpreted as a material time, i.e.:94\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{f}_{,\tilde{p}} < 0 \quad (\text {H}_2) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f¯,p~<0(H2)It follows the property:

Property 6

Under the (H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}2) hypothesis and if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}$$\end{document}f¯ is convex and piecewise regular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}$$\end{document}f¯ can be replaced by:95\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}}) - \tilde{p} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a(σσ,X~)-p~where a is a convex function such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(0,\textbf{0})\le 0$$\end{document}a(0,0)≤0.

Proof

From \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})=0$$\end{document}f¯(σσ,X~,p~)=0, one gets96\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{p} = a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p~=a(σσ,X~)and we have:97\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{f}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}}))=0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f¯(σσ,X~,a(σσ,X~))=0The second differential of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}}))$$\end{document}f¯(σσ,X~,a(σσ,X~)) is:98\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0=[δ2f¯](σσ,X~,p~)p~=a(σσ,X~)+∂f∂p[δ2a](σσ,X~)It follows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Moreover, from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}(0,\textbf{0},0)\le 0$$\end{document}f¯(0,0,0)≤0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \ge 0$$\end{document}p≥0, one gets:99\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a(0,\textbf{0}) \le 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a(0,0)≤0Finally, the data-driven mathematical model compatible with the knowledge from physics and materials science is defined by the convex function a alone, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(0,\textbf{0}) \le 0$$\end{document}a(0,0)≤0. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 3

Some more assumptions can be introduced for particular materials. For example, for metallic materials, the plastic incompressibility is commonly used. Isotropy or orthotropy properties can also be added and allow important complexity reduction.

It follows that the dual potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi $$\end{document}ϕ is:100\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \phi (\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}})&= \sup _{\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p},a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}}) \le \tilde{p}}\left[ \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p] - \tilde{\textbf{X}}\cdot \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}} - \tilde{p}.\dot{\tilde{p}} \right] \\&= \sup _{\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p},a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}}) \le \tilde{p}}\left[ \dot{\tilde{p}}(\text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p/\dot{\tilde{p}}] - \tilde{\textbf{X}}\cdot \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}}/\dot{\tilde{p}}-a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}})+\dot{\tilde{p}}(a-\tilde{p}) \right] \qquad \\&= \dot{\tilde{p}} a^*(\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p/\dot{\tilde{p}},-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}}/\dot{\tilde{p}}) + 0 \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙)=supσσ,X~,p~,a(σσ,X~)≤p~Tr[σσϵϵ˙p]-X~·X~˙-p~.p~˙=supσσ,X~,p~,a(σσ,X~)≤p~p~˙(Tr[σσϵϵ˙p/p~˙]-X~·X~˙/p~˙-a(σσ,X~)+p~˙(a-p~)=p~˙a∗(ϵϵ˙p/p~˙,-X~˙/p~˙)+0The local CRE can be written:101\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e^2_{CRE} = \dot{\tilde{p}} \left[ a^*(\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p/\dot{\tilde{p}},-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}}/\dot{\tilde{p}}) + a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}}) - \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p/\dot{\tilde{p}}] + \tilde{\textbf{X}} \cdot \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}}/\dot{\tilde{p}}\right] + \dot{\tilde{p}}\left[ \tilde{p} - a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}})\right] \nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}eCRE2=p~˙a∗(ϵϵ˙p/p~˙,-X~˙/p~˙)+a(σσ,X~)-Tr[σσϵϵ˙p/p~˙]+X~·X~˙/p~˙+p~˙p~-a(σσ,X~)where the quantities between brackets are positive or null.

Standard material model for elasto-viscoplastic materials

A common assumption in materials science is to consider viscoplasticity as an extension or regularization of plasticity. The elastic domain being described by the convex function a, the potential is defined as:102\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p}) = \gamma (\langle a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}})-\tilde{p}\rangle _+) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσ,X~,p~)=γ(⟨a(σσ,X~)-p~⟩+)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ is an increasing convex function defined over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}^+$$\end{document}R+ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma (0)=0$$\end{document}γ(0)=0. The potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\phi ^*}$$\end{document}ϕ∗ is thus convex, positive and satisfies:103\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\phi ^*}(0,\textbf{0},0) = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗(0,0,0)=0To get the dual potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\phi }$$\end{document}ϕ, one has to take the Legendre-Fenchel transform of a combination of convex functions named composite convex functions for which results are given in [36]. Here, one has:104\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {\phi }(\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}})&= \sup _{\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p} \in \textbf{R}^{6+q} \times \textbf{R}^+} \left[ \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p] - Illustration: back to the normal/standard model of Chaboche-Marquis

We have seen that the potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D,\tilde{\mathbb {X}},\tilde{p})$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσD,X~,p~) is equal to:106\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D,\tilde{\mathbb {X}},\tilde{p}) = \frac{k}{n+1}\langle z \rangle _+^{n+1} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσD,X~,p~)=kn+1⟨z⟩+n+1with107\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} z = |\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D - One also has:109\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D,\tilde{\mathbb {X}},\tilde{p}) = \gamma (\langle a(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }_D,\tilde{\mathbb {X}})-\tilde{p}\rangle _+) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσD,X~,p~)=γ(⟨a(σσD,X~)-p~⟩+)with110\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \gamma (r) = \frac{k}{n+1}[\ell (r)]^{n+1} \quad \forall r \in \textbf{R}^+ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}γ(r)=kn+1[ℓ(r)]n+1∀r∈R+

Optimal solution of standard ill-posed problems

Let us consider that the behavior of the material is known and defined by the potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\mathbb {X}},\tilde{p})$$\end{document}ϕ∗(σσ,X~,p~) which is a convex function such that:111\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\phi ^*} \ge 0 ; \quad {\phi ^*}(0,\textbf{0},0) = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∗≥0;ϕ∗(0,0,0)=0The displacement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{U}^K$$\end{document}U_K and the stress \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S$$\end{document}σσS defined over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,T] \times \Omega $$\end{document}[0,T]×Ω should be admissible, i.e.:112\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{U}^K \in \varvec{\mathcal {U}}^{K,[0,T]}_{ad} ; \quad \sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S \in \varvec{\mathcal {S}}^{S,[0,T]}_{ad} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U_K∈UadK,[0,T];σσS∈SadS,[0,T]and the problem to solve is a standard ill-posed problem. A solution could be given by the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\underline{U}^K,\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S)$$\end{document}(U_K,σσS) which minimizes the global CRE functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{CRE}$$\end{document}ECRE over the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\mathcal {U}}^{K,[0,T]}_{ad} \times \varvec{\mathcal {S}}^{S,[0,T]}_{ad}$$\end{document}UadK,[0,T]×SadS,[0,T]. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{CRE}$$\end{document}ECRE being a strongly convex function for standard material models, the solution is unique. Here, the optimal solution is computed.

First we construct a solution defined by a modified potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\phi ^*_M}$$\end{document}ϕM∗ and in a second time, we prove that it is optimal. For elasto-(visco-)plastic materials, the modified constitutive relation is also elasto-(visco-)plastic but nonlocal; however, the optimal solution can be computed without difficulty thanks to an incremental numerical technique.

A remarkable constraint

We will see that for standard ill-posed problems, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p$$\end{document}ϵϵ˙p is not a priori arbitrary. One has:113\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p = \dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}^K - \textbf{K}^{-1}\dot{\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }}^S \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵϵ˙p=ϵϵ˙K-K-1σσ˙Sand the question is to compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }^K$$\end{document}ϵϵK and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S$$\end{document}σσS from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p$$\end{document}ϵϵ˙p. First, let us compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S$$\end{document}σσS; it is defined for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in ]0,T[$$\end{document}t∈]0,T[ by:114\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }^S \in \varvec{\mathcal {S}}_{ad}^S \;; To get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{U}^K = \underline{U}$$\end{document}U_K=U_, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{U}$$\end{document}U_ should also satisfy:117\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{U}= \underline{U}_d \; Property 7

For standard ill-posed problems, the inelastic strain satisfies the constraint:120\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Omega }\text {Tr}[\textbf{K}\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_p \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }(\underline{W}^*)]\text {d}\Omega = \int _{\Omega }\text {Tr}[\textbf{K}\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e^{KS}\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }(\underline{W}^*)]\text {d}\Omega \quad \forall \underline{W}^*\in \varvec{\mathcal {W}}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫ΩTr[Kϵϵpϵϵ(W_∗)]dΩ=∫ΩTr[KϵϵeKSϵϵ(W_∗)]dΩ∀W_∗∈Wwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e^{KS} \equiv \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e^K - \epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e^S$$\end{document}ϵϵeKS≡ϵϵeK-ϵϵeS, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e^K$$\end{document}ϵϵeK and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }_e^S$$\end{document}ϵϵeS being the elastic solutions for the K-data and the S-data, respectively.

Remark 4

For a well-posed computational problem, the constraint (120) disappears.

Modified potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Phi ^*_M}$$\end{document}ΦM∗

Let us introduce the global potentials:121\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\Phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p}) = \int _{\Omega }{\phi ^*}(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})\text {d}\Omega ; \quad {\Phi }(\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}}) = \int _{\Omega }{\phi }(\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}}) \text {d}\Omega \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ∗(σσ,X~,p~)=∫Ωϕ∗(σσ,X~,p~)dΩ;Φ(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙)=∫Ωϕ(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙)dΩOne considers first the case where the reference potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Phi ^*}$$\end{document}Φ∗ is regular. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{CRE}$$\end{document}ECRE-minimization leads for fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma },\tilde{\textbf{X}},\tilde{p})$$\end{document}(σσ,X~,p~) to the minimization of:122\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\Phi }(\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p,-\dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}},-\dot{\tilde{p}}) - \int _{\Omega }\left[ \text {Tr}[\sigma \hspace{-5.0pt}{\sigma }\dot{\epsilon \hspace{-2.5pt}{\epsilon }}_p] - \tilde{\textbf{X}}\cdot \dot{\tilde{\textbf{X}}} - \tilde{p}.\dot{\tilde{p}}\right] \text {d}\Omega \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙)+χHp(ϵϵ˙p,-X~˙,-p~˙)-∫ΩTr[σσϵϵ˙p]-X~·X~˙-p~.p~˙dΩwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\upchi _{\textbf{H}_p}$$\end{document}χHp is the characteristic function of the subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}_p$$\end{document}Hp. It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek}
